But I also worry constantly that everyone else is judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, which makes any social outing where photos might be taken feel like a total minefield.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a real defect or flaw in your appearance that others would also notice, or do you mainly worry about being judged or embarrassed in social situations?\n\nPatient: I absolutely believe it is a real physical defect, as my dark circles and bloated, uneven face are incredibly obvious to me. I am convinced that others are noticing these flaws and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, even though my friends and coworkers claim they don't see anything wrong. It feels like a very real, visible problem rather than just a general worry about being judged."
  }

I am just so consumed by how awful I look that I'd rather skip an event entirely than risk being photographed, even if my friends tell me they don't notice anything.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day do you spend thinking about or checking these appearance concerns, and do you do things like mirror checking, comparing photos, or seeking reassurance because of them?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I spend hours every day fixating on this, often replaying images of my face in my head for hours. Before any video meeting or event, I spend 30 to 40 minutes adjusting the lighting, angles, and my makeup to hide my puffy face. During and after calls, I constantly check my camera box, zoom in on flaws in tagged photos, and ask my friends for reassurance that I didn't look terrible.

It feels like a very real, visible problem rather than just a general worry about being judged.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social situations, is it mainly because you fear people will judge or embarrass you in general, or because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaw itself even when no one else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I am so preoccupied with these specific flaws, like my eye bags and bloated face, rather than a general fear of social situations. I am just so consumed by how awful I look that I'd rather skip an event entirely than risk being photographed, even if my friends tell me they don't notice anything.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day do you spend thinking about or checking these appearance concerns, and do you do things like mirror checking, comparing photos, or seeking reassurance because of them?\n\nPatient: I spend hours every day fixating on this, often replaying images of my face in my head for hours. Before any video meeting or event, I spend 30 to 40 minutes adjusting the lighting, angles, and my makeup to hide my puffy face. During and after calls, I constantly check my camera box, zoom in on flaws in tagged photos, and ask my friends for reassurance that I didn't look terrible.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social or work situations, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in general, or because you are preoccupied with a specific flaw in your appearance even when other people say they do not notice it?\n\nPatient: My avoidance is entirely because of my preoccupation with my dark circles and puffy face, not a general fear of embarrassment. Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all."

A couple of offhand comments from coworkers about looking \"tired\" on screen and a particularly unflattering still frame from a recorded webinar stuck with her, and she began rearranging her schedule and declining opportunities that involved being on video or photographed at community tech events. Her active social life—dancing, soccer, festivals—started to feel more like a minefield of situations where she might be tagged in photos or appear on social media, and she found herself hesitating to join outings she once organized. When she missed a chance at a promotion because she declined to lead a highly visible training series, and her manager gently raised concerns about her sudden avoidance of public-facing tasks, she finally agreed to see a psychiatrist at the urging of a close friend from her Latino community group.\n- Chief Complaint: “I can’t stop fixating on how awful I look on camera and in photos, and it’s starting to affect my work and social life.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S067 (Preoccupation_with_Appearance): Whenever I see myself on Zoom or in a photo, all I can focus on are these dark circles and the way my face looks bloated and uneven, and I replay that image in my head for hours, convinced everyone must be thinking I look exhausted and sloppy even if they say they don’t notice anything wrong.\n- S068 (Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors): Before any video call or event where people might take pictures, I’ll spend 30–40 minutes adjusting the lighting, trying different angles, redoing my makeup to hide what I see as my puffy face and eye bags, and then constantly checking my little camera box or tagged photos afterward, zooming in on every tiny flaw and asking my friends if I really looked that bad.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve turned down leading webinars and passed on a promotion because it meant being on camera all the time, and I’ve also started skipping parties and soccer games if I think people might take photos, so my work progress and social life have both really stalled.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “I can’t stop fixating on how awful I look on camera and in photos, and it’s starting to affect my work and social life.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I can’t stop fixating on how awful I look on camera and in photos, and it’s starting to affect my work and social life. It has gotten to the point where I'm avoiding important parts of my job and staying home from social outings, which is why a close friend urged me to come speak with you.\n\nDoctor: Besides worries about how you look, do you also spend a lot of time feeling down or tense about many different things in life, or is the main problem specifically your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel anxious or down about other things in my life; the main problem is specifically how I look. All of my stress and avoidance are tied directly to seeing my dark circles, eye bags, and how bloated and uneven my face looks on screen. When I'm not focused on that, I'm generally fine, but this one thing has just completely taken over.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations mainly because you fear other people will judge your appearance, or is the concern mostly about your own distress when you see yourself in photos or on camera?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. I feel incredibly distressed when I see my dark circles and bloated face on camera, and I'll replay those images in my head for hours afterward. But I also worry constantly that everyone else is judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, which makes any social outing where photos might be taken feel like a total minefield.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a real defect or flaw in your appearance that others would also notice, or do you mainly worry about being judged or embarrassed in social situations?\n\nPatient: I absolutely believe it is a real physical defect, as my dark circles and bloated, uneven face are incredibly obvious to me. I am convinced that others are noticing these flaws and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, even though my friends and coworkers claim they don't see anything wrong. It feels like a very real, visible problem rather than just a general worry about being judged.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social situations, is it mainly because you fear people will judge or embarrass you in general, or because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaw itself even when no one else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I am so preoccupied with these specific flaws, like my eye bags and bloated face, rather than a general fear of social situations. I am just so consumed by how awful I look that I'd rather skip an event entirely than risk being photographed, even if my friends tell me they don't notice anything.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day do you spend thinking about or checking these appearance concerns, and do you do things like mirror checking, comparing photos, or seeking reassurance because of them?\n\nPatient: I spend hours every day fixating on this, often replaying images of my face in my head for hours. Before any video meeting or event, I spend 30 to 40 minutes adjusting the lighting, angles, and my makeup to hide my puffy face. During and after calls, I constantly check my camera box, zoom in on flaws in tagged photos, and ask my friends for reassurance that I didn't look terrible.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social or work situations, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in general, or because you are preoccupied with a specific flaw in your appearance even when other people say they do not notice it?\n\nPatient: My avoidance is entirely because of my preoccupation with my dark circles and puffy face, not a general fear of embarrassment. Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social or work situations mainly because you fear being judged or embarrassed by other people in general, or is it mostly because of a specific concern about your appearance even when others say they do not notice a problem?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the latter, as my avoidance is entirely driven by my obsession with how my face looks, specifically my dark circles and bloated appearance. Even when my friends and colleagues reassure me that they don't notice anything wrong, I still can't stop fixating on these flaws. That is why I avoid these situations, rather than having a general fear of people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the problem is more about being judged or embarrassed in social situations in general, even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only about the specific flaw in your appearance?"

I actually don't have a general fear of social situations at all—I used to be very active with dancing and soccer, and I only started skipping those outings because I'm terrified of being photographed or tagged on social media.

I am just so consumed by how awful I look that I'd rather skip an event entirely than risk being photographed, even if my friends tell me they don't notice anything.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day do you spend thinking about or checking these appearance concerns, and do you do things like mirror checking, comparing photos, or seeking reassurance because of them?\n\nPatient: I spend hours every day fixating on this, often replaying images of my face in my head for hours. Before any video meeting or event, I spend 30 to 40 minutes adjusting the lighting, angles, and my makeup to hide my puffy face. During and after calls, I constantly check my camera box, zoom in on flaws in tagged photos, and ask my friends for reassurance that I didn't look terrible.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social or work situations, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in general, or because you are preoccupied with a specific flaw in your appearance even when other people say they do not notice it?\n\nPatient: My avoidance is entirely because of my preoccupation with my dark circles and puffy face, not a general fear of embarrassment. Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social or work situations mainly because you fear being judged or embarrassed by other people in general, or is it mostly because of a specific concern about your appearance even when others say they do not notice a problem?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the latter, as my avoidance is entirely driven by my obsession with how my face looks, specifically my dark circles and bloated appearance. Even when my friends and colleagues reassure me that they don't notice anything wrong, I still can't stop fixating on these flaws. That is why I avoid these situations, rather than having a general fear of people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the problem is more about being judged or embarrassed in social situations in general, even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only about the specific flaw in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, it is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance, like my bloated face and dark circles. I actually don't have a general fear of social situations at all—I used to be very active with dancing and soccer, and I only started skipping those outings because I'm terrified of being photographed or tagged on social media.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that you have a serious medical or physical illness because of these appearance concerns, or is the problem mainly that you think your appearance is flawed and others will notice it?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't worry about having a medical illness; the issue is purely aesthetic. I am just deeply self-conscious about looking bloated and uneven with these dark circles, and I constantly worry that others will notice and judge me for looking exhausted and sloppy.

It has gotten to the point where I'm avoiding important parts of my job and staying home from social outings, which is why a close friend urged me to come speak with you.\n\nDoctor: Besides worries about how you look, do you also spend a lot of time feeling down or tense about many different things in life, or is the main problem specifically your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel anxious or down about other things in my life; the main problem is specifically how I look. All of my stress and avoidance are tied directly to seeing my dark circles, eye bags, and how bloated and uneven my face looks on screen. When I'm not focused on that, I'm generally fine, but this one thing has just completely taken over.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations mainly because you fear other people will judge your appearance, or is the concern mostly about your own distress when you see yourself in photos or on camera?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. I feel incredibly distressed when I see my dark circles and bloated face on camera, and I'll replay those images in my head for hours afterward. But I also worry constantly that everyone else is judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, which makes any social outing where photos might be taken feel like a total minefield.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a real defect or flaw in your appearance that others would also notice, or do you mainly worry about being judged or embarrassed in social situations?\n\nPatient: I absolutely believe it is a real physical defect, as my dark circles and bloated, uneven face are incredibly obvious to me. I am convinced that others are noticing these flaws and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, even though my friends and coworkers claim they don't see anything wrong. It feels like a very real, visible problem rather than just a general worry about being judged.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social situations, is it mainly because you fear people will judge or embarrass you in general, or because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaw itself even when no one else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I am so preoccupied with these specific flaws, like my eye bags and bloated face, rather than a general fear of social situations. I am just so consumed by how awful I look that I'd rather skip an event entirely than risk being photographed, even if my friends tell me they don't notice anything.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day do you spend thinking about or checking these appearance concerns, and do you do things like mirror checking, comparing photos, or seeking reassurance because of them?\n\nPatient: I spend hours every day fixating on this, often replaying images of my face in my head for hours. Before any video meeting or event, I spend 30 to 40 minutes adjusting the lighting, angles, and my makeup to hide my puffy face. During and after calls, I constantly check my camera box, zoom in on flaws in tagged photos, and ask my friends for reassurance that I didn't look terrible.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social or work situations, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in general, or because you are preoccupied with a specific flaw in your appearance even when other people say they do not notice it?\n\nPatient: My avoidance is entirely because of my preoccupation with my dark circles and puffy face, not a general fear of embarrassment. Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social or work situations mainly because you fear being judged or embarrassed by other people in general, or is it mostly because of a specific concern about your appearance even when others say they do not notice a problem?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the latter, as my avoidance is entirely driven by my obsession with how my face looks, specifically my dark circles and bloated appearance. Even when my friends and colleagues reassure me that they don't notice anything wrong, I still can't stop fixating on these flaws. That is why I avoid these situations, rather than having a general fear of people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the problem is more about being judged or embarrassed in social situations in general, even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only about the specific flaw in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, it is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance, like my bloated face and dark circles. I actually don't have a general fear of social situations at all—I used to be very active with dancing and soccer, and I only started skipping those outings because I'm terrified of being photographed or tagged on social media.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that you have a serious medical or physical illness because of these appearance concerns, or is the problem mainly that you think your appearance is flawed and others will notice it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness; the issue is purely aesthetic. I am just deeply self-conscious about looking bloated and uneven with these dark circles, and I constantly worry that others will notice and judge me for looking exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations because you feel awkward or afraid of being judged even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only because of the specific flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel awkward or anxious in social situations for any other reason; it is strictly because of how I look. I only avoid these events because I am terrified of people seeing my bloated face and dark circles, or being captured in an unflattering photo."

Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social or work situations mainly because you fear being judged or embarrassed by other people in general, or is it mostly because of a specific concern about your appearance even when others say they do not notice a problem?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the latter, as my avoidance is entirely driven by my obsession with how my face looks, specifically my dark circles and bloated appearance. Even when my friends and colleagues reassure me that they don't notice anything wrong, I still can't stop fixating on these flaws. That is why I avoid these situations, rather than having a general fear of people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the problem is more about being judged or embarrassed in social situations in general, even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only about the specific flaw in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, it is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance, like my bloated face and dark circles. I actually don't have a general fear of social situations at all—I used to be very active with dancing and soccer, and I only started skipping those outings because I'm terrified of being photographed or tagged on social media.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that you have a serious medical or physical illness because of these appearance concerns, or is the problem mainly that you think your appearance is flawed and others will notice it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness; the issue is purely aesthetic. I am just deeply self-conscious about looking bloated and uneven with these dark circles, and I constantly worry that others will notice and judge me for looking exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations because you feel awkward or afraid of being judged even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only because of the specific flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel awkward or anxious in social situations for any other reason; it is strictly because of how I look. I only avoid these events because I am terrified of people seeing my bloated face and dark circles, or being captured in an unflattering photo.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid events, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in social situations in general, or is it almost entirely because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: It is almost entirely because of my preoccupation with my specific flaws, like my dark circles and bloated, uneven face, and the dread of being captured on camera. I don't have a general fear of social situations at all. I just can't bear the thought of people seeing or photographing these parts of my face."
  }

I only avoid these events because I am terrified of people seeing my bloated face and dark circles, or being captured in an unflattering photo.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid events, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in social situations in general, or is it almost entirely because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: It is almost entirely because of my preoccupation with my specific flaws, like my dark circles and bloated, uneven face, and the dread of being captured on camera. I don't have a general fear of social situations at all. I just can't bear the thought of people seeing or photographing these parts of my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to others, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that the dark circles and the bloated, uneven look of my face are very real and highly visible. I just can't help but feel that everyone is secretly judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy."

I am just deeply self-conscious about looking bloated and uneven with these dark circles, and I constantly worry that others will notice and judge me for looking exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations because you feel awkward or afraid of being judged even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only because of the specific flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel awkward or anxious in social situations for any other reason; it is strictly because of how I look. I only avoid these events because I am terrified of people seeing my bloated face and dark circles, or being captured in an unflattering photo.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid events, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in social situations in general, or is it almost entirely because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: It is almost entirely because of my preoccupation with my specific flaws, like my dark circles and bloated, uneven face, and the dread of being captured on camera. I don't have a general fear of social situations at all. I just can't bear the thought of people seeing or photographing these parts of my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to others, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that the dark circles and the bloated, uneven look of my face are very real and highly visible. I just can't help but feel that everyone is secretly judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a strong fear of being embarrassed or judged in social situations even when your appearance is not being noticed, or is your distress only about the specific flaw you see in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, my distress is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance. I don't have a general fear of being judged or embarrassed in social situations at all. My anxiety and avoidance, like skipping soccer or parties, are purely driven by the fear of being photographed or having others notice my dark circles and bloated face."

Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social or work situations mainly because you fear being judged or embarrassed by other people in general, or is it mostly because of a specific concern about your appearance even when others say they do not notice a problem?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the latter, as my avoidance is entirely driven by my obsession with how my face looks, specifically my dark circles and bloated appearance. Even when my friends and colleagues reassure me that they don't notice anything wrong, I still can't stop fixating on these flaws. That is why I avoid these situations, rather than having a general fear of people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the problem is more about being judged or embarrassed in social situations in general, even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only about the specific flaw in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, it is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance, like my bloated face and dark circles. I actually don't have a general fear of social situations at all—I used to be very active with dancing and soccer, and I only started skipping those outings because I'm terrified of being photographed or tagged on social media.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that you have a serious medical or physical illness because of these appearance concerns, or is the problem mainly that you think your appearance is flawed and others will notice it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness; the issue is purely aesthetic. I am just deeply self-conscious about looking bloated and uneven with these dark circles, and I constantly worry that others will notice and judge me for looking exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations because you feel awkward or afraid of being judged even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only because of the specific flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel awkward or anxious in social situations for any other reason; it is strictly because of how I look. I only avoid these events because I am terrified of people seeing my bloated face and dark circles, or being captured in an unflattering photo.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid events, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in social situations in general, or is it almost entirely because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: It is almost entirely because of my preoccupation with my specific flaws, like my dark circles and bloated, uneven face, and the dread of being captured on camera. I don't have a general fear of social situations at all. I just can't bear the thought of people seeing or photographing these parts of my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to others, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that the dark circles and the bloated, uneven look of my face are very real and highly visible. I just can't help but feel that everyone is secretly judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a strong fear of being embarrassed or judged in social situations even when your appearance is not being noticed, or is your distress only about the specific flaw you see in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, my distress is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance. I don't have a general fear of being judged or embarrassed in social situations at all. My anxiety and avoidance, like skipping soccer or parties, are purely driven by the fear of being photographed or having others notice my dark circles and bloated face.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious illness or disease because of these symptoms, or is your concern mainly that your appearance looks flawed or unattractive?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness or disease at all. My concern is entirely that my appearance looks flawed, bloated, and uneven, which makes me look incredibly unattractive and exhausted on camera. I am just deeply distressed by how I look in photos, not by any fear of being sick."

When I'm not focused on that, I'm generally fine, but this one thing has just completely taken over.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations mainly because you fear other people will judge your appearance, or is the concern mostly about your own distress when you see yourself in photos or on camera?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. I feel incredibly distressed when I see my dark circles and bloated face on camera, and I'll replay those images in my head for hours afterward. But I also worry constantly that everyone else is judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, which makes any social outing where photos might be taken feel like a total minefield.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a real defect or flaw in your appearance that others would also notice, or do you mainly worry about being judged or embarrassed in social situations?\n\nPatient: I absolutely believe it is a real physical defect, as my dark circles and bloated, uneven face are incredibly obvious to me. I am convinced that others are noticing these flaws and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, even though my friends and coworkers claim they don't see anything wrong. It feels like a very real, visible problem rather than just a general worry about being judged.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social situations, is it mainly because you fear people will judge or embarrass you in general, or because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaw itself even when no one else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I am so preoccupied with these specific flaws, like my eye bags and bloated face, rather than a general fear of social situations. I am just so consumed by how awful I look that I'd rather skip an event entirely than risk being photographed, even if my friends tell me they don't notice anything.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day do you spend thinking about or checking these appearance concerns, and do you do things like mirror checking, comparing photos, or seeking reassurance because of them?\n\nPatient: I spend hours every day fixating on this, often replaying images of my face in my head for hours. Before any video meeting or event, I spend 30 to 40 minutes adjusting the lighting, angles, and my makeup to hide my puffy face. During and after calls, I constantly check my camera box, zoom in on flaws in tagged photos, and ask my friends for reassurance that I didn't look terrible.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social or work situations, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in general, or because you are preoccupied with a specific flaw in your appearance even when other people say they do not notice it?\n\nPatient: My avoidance is entirely because of my preoccupation with my dark circles and puffy face, not a general fear of embarrassment. Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social or work situations mainly because you fear being judged or embarrassed by other people in general, or is it mostly because of a specific concern about your appearance even when others say they do not notice a problem?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the latter, as my avoidance is entirely driven by my obsession with how my face looks, specifically my dark circles and bloated appearance. Even when my friends and colleagues reassure me that they don't notice anything wrong, I still can't stop fixating on these flaws. That is why I avoid these situations, rather than having a general fear of people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the problem is more about being judged or embarrassed in social situations in general, even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only about the specific flaw in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, it is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance, like my bloated face and dark circles. I actually don't have a general fear of social situations at all—I used to be very active with dancing and soccer, and I only started skipping those outings because I'm terrified of being photographed or tagged on social media.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that you have a serious medical or physical illness because of these appearance concerns, or is the problem mainly that you think your appearance is flawed and others will notice it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness; the issue is purely aesthetic. I am just deeply self-conscious about looking bloated and uneven with these dark circles, and I constantly worry that others will notice and judge me for looking exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations because you feel awkward or afraid of being judged even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only because of the specific flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel awkward or anxious in social situations for any other reason; it is strictly because of how I look. I only avoid these events because I am terrified of people seeing my bloated face and dark circles, or being captured in an unflattering photo.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid events, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in social situations in general, or is it almost entirely because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: It is almost entirely because of my preoccupation with my specific flaws, like my dark circles and bloated, uneven face, and the dread of being captured on camera. I don't have a general fear of social situations at all. I just can't bear the thought of people seeing or photographing these parts of my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to others, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that the dark circles and the bloated, uneven look of my face are very real and highly visible. I just can't help but feel that everyone is secretly judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a strong fear of being embarrassed or judged in social situations even when your appearance is not being noticed, or is your distress only about the specific flaw you see in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, my distress is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance. I don't have a general fear of being judged or embarrassed in social situations at all. My anxiety and avoidance, like skipping soccer or parties, are purely driven by the fear of being photographed or having others notice my dark circles and bloated face.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious illness or disease because of these symptoms, or is your concern mainly that your appearance looks flawed or unattractive?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness or disease at all. My concern is entirely that my appearance looks flawed, bloated, and uneven, which makes me look incredibly unattractive and exhausted on camera. I am just deeply distressed by how I look in photos, not by any fear of being sick.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is even a small chance that your concern about your face may be exaggerated or not as visible to others as it feels to you, or are you completely certain the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my bloated face and dark circles are real and highly obvious. When I look in the mirror or on camera, it feels absolutely undeniable, and I often worry that others are just trying to be nice when they say they don't see it."
  }
]

However, the patient is also completely convinced the flaw is real and obvious despite contrary evidence, with fixed conviction that others are secretly judging them, so a delusional-spectrum code is now also plausible. The list has therefore expanded to include both possibilities pending final clarification of whether the belief is overvalued/preoccupying versus fully delusional.","is_final":false}

My anxiety and avoidance, like skipping soccer or parties, are purely driven by the fear of being photographed or having others notice my dark circles and bloated face.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious illness or disease because of these symptoms, or is your concern mainly that your appearance looks flawed or unattractive?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness or disease at all. My concern is entirely that my appearance looks flawed, bloated, and uneven, which makes me look incredibly unattractive and exhausted on camera. I am just deeply distressed by how I look in photos, not by any fear of being sick.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is even a small chance that your concern about your face may be exaggerated or not as visible to others as it feels to you, or are you completely certain the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my bloated face and dark circles are real and highly obvious. When I look in the mirror or on camera, it feels absolutely undeniable, and I often worry that others are just trying to be nice when they say they don't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance that your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to other people, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Even though others tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my dark circles and bloated face are real and highly obvious. I constantly replay those images in my head, certain that everyone thinks I look exhausted and sloppy, which is why I keep asking my friends for reassurance.

It feels like a very real, visible problem rather than just a general worry about being judged.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social situations, is it mainly because you fear people will judge or embarrass you in general, or because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaw itself even when no one else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I am so preoccupied with these specific flaws, like my eye bags and bloated face, rather than a general fear of social situations. I am just so consumed by how awful I look that I'd rather skip an event entirely than risk being photographed, even if my friends tell me they don't notice anything.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day do you spend thinking about or checking these appearance concerns, and do you do things like mirror checking, comparing photos, or seeking reassurance because of them?\n\nPatient: I spend hours every day fixating on this, often replaying images of my face in my head for hours. Before any video meeting or event, I spend 30 to 40 minutes adjusting the lighting, angles, and my makeup to hide my puffy face. During and after calls, I constantly check my camera box, zoom in on flaws in tagged photos, and ask my friends for reassurance that I didn't look terrible.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social or work situations, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in general, or because you are preoccupied with a specific flaw in your appearance even when other people say they do not notice it?\n\nPatient: My avoidance is entirely because of my preoccupation with my dark circles and puffy face, not a general fear of embarrassment. Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social or work situations mainly because you fear being judged or embarrassed by other people in general, or is it mostly because of a specific concern about your appearance even when others say they do not notice a problem?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the latter, as my avoidance is entirely driven by my obsession with how my face looks, specifically my dark circles and bloated appearance. Even when my friends and colleagues reassure me that they don't notice anything wrong, I still can't stop fixating on these flaws. That is why I avoid these situations, rather than having a general fear of people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the problem is more about being judged or embarrassed in social situations in general, even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only about the specific flaw in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, it is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance, like my bloated face and dark circles. I actually don't have a general fear of social situations at all—I used to be very active with dancing and soccer, and I only started skipping those outings because I'm terrified of being photographed or tagged on social media.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that you have a serious medical or physical illness because of these appearance concerns, or is the problem mainly that you think your appearance is flawed and others will notice it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness; the issue is purely aesthetic. I am just deeply self-conscious about looking bloated and uneven with these dark circles, and I constantly worry that others will notice and judge me for looking exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations because you feel awkward or afraid of being judged even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only because of the specific flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel awkward or anxious in social situations for any other reason; it is strictly because of how I look. I only avoid these events because I am terrified of people seeing my bloated face and dark circles, or being captured in an unflattering photo.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid events, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in social situations in general, or is it almost entirely because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: It is almost entirely because of my preoccupation with my specific flaws, like my dark circles and bloated, uneven face, and the dread of being captured on camera. I don't have a general fear of social situations at all. I just can't bear the thought of people seeing or photographing these parts of my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to others, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that the dark circles and the bloated, uneven look of my face are very real and highly visible. I just can't help but feel that everyone is secretly judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a strong fear of being embarrassed or judged in social situations even when your appearance is not being noticed, or is your distress only about the specific flaw you see in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, my distress is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance. I don't have a general fear of being judged or embarrassed in social situations at all. My anxiety and avoidance, like skipping soccer or parties, are purely driven by the fear of being photographed or having others notice my dark circles and bloated face.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious illness or disease because of these symptoms, or is your concern mainly that your appearance looks flawed or unattractive?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness or disease at all. My concern is entirely that my appearance looks flawed, bloated, and uneven, which makes me look incredibly unattractive and exhausted on camera. I am just deeply distressed by how I look in photos, not by any fear of being sick.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is even a small chance that your concern about your face may be exaggerated or not as visible to others as it feels to you, or are you completely certain the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my bloated face and dark circles are real and highly obvious. When I look in the mirror or on camera, it feels absolutely undeniable, and I often worry that others are just trying to be nice when they say they don't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance that your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to other people, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though others tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my dark circles and bloated face are real and highly obvious. I constantly replay those images in my head, certain that everyone thinks I look exhausted and sloppy, which is why I keep asking my friends for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that other people are deliberately talking about, mocking, or targeting you because of your appearance, or is your concern limited to being very distressed about how your face looks?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking or targeting me maliciously. I am just constantly convinced that anyone looking at me must be thinking I look exhausted, bloated, and sloppy because of my dark circles and uneven face. My distress is entirely about those specific flaws and the fear of being judged for them.

Her active social life—dancing, soccer, festivals—started to feel more like a minefield of situations where she might be tagged in photos or appear on social media, and she found herself hesitating to join outings she once organized. When she missed a chance at a promotion because she declined to lead a highly visible training series, and her manager gently raised concerns about her sudden avoidance of public-facing tasks, she finally agreed to see a psychiatrist at the urging of a close friend from her Latino community group.\n- Chief Complaint: “I can’t stop fixating on how awful I look on camera and in photos, and it’s starting to affect my work and social life.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S067 (Preoccupation_with_Appearance): Whenever I see myself on Zoom or in a photo, all I can focus on are these dark circles and the way my face looks bloated and uneven, and I replay that image in my head for hours, convinced everyone must be thinking I look exhausted and sloppy even if they say they don’t notice anything wrong.\n- S068 (Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors): Before any video call or event where people might take pictures, I’ll spend 30–40 minutes adjusting the lighting, trying different angles, redoing my makeup to hide what I see as my puffy face and eye bags, and then constantly checking my little camera box or tagged photos afterward, zooming in on every tiny flaw and asking my friends if I really looked that bad.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve turned down leading webinars and passed on a promotion because it meant being on camera all the time, and I’ve also started skipping parties and soccer games if I think people might take photos, so my work progress and social life have both really stalled.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint.     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I can’t stop fixating on how awful I look on camera and in photos, and it’s starting to affect my work and social life. It has gotten to the point where I'm avoiding important parts of my job and staying home from social outings, which is why a close friend urged me to come speak with you.\n\nDoctor: Besides worries about how you look, do you also spend a lot of time feeling down or tense about many different things in life, or is the main problem specifically your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel anxious or down about other things in my life; the main problem is specifically how I look. All of my stress and avoidance are tied directly to seeing my dark circles, eye bags, and how bloated and uneven my face looks on screen. When I'm not focused on that, I'm generally fine, but this one thing has just completely taken over.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social situations mainly because you fear other people will judge your appearance, or is the concern mostly about your own distress when you see yourself in photos or on camera?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. I feel incredibly distressed when I see my dark circles and bloated face on camera, and I'll replay those images in my head for hours afterward. But I also worry constantly that everyone else is judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, which makes any social outing where photos might be taken feel like a total minefield.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is a real defect or flaw in your appearance that others would also notice, or do you mainly worry about being judged or embarrassed in social situations?\n\nPatient: I absolutely believe it is a real physical defect, as my dark circles and bloated, uneven face are incredibly obvious to me. I am convinced that others are noticing these flaws and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy, even though my friends and coworkers claim they don't see anything wrong. It feels like a very real, visible problem rather than just a general worry about being judged.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social situations, is it mainly because you fear people will judge or embarrass you in general, or because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaw itself even when no one else seems to notice it?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely because I am so preoccupied with these specific flaws, like my eye bags and bloated face, rather than a general fear of social situations. I am just so consumed by how awful I look that I'd rather skip an event entirely than risk being photographed, even if my friends tell me they don't notice anything.\n\nDoctor: How much time each day do you spend thinking about or checking these appearance concerns, and do you do things like mirror checking, comparing photos, or seeking reassurance because of them?\n\nPatient: I spend hours every day fixating on this, often replaying images of my face in my head for hours. Before any video meeting or event, I spend 30 to 40 minutes adjusting the lighting, angles, and my makeup to hide my puffy face. During and after calls, I constantly check my camera box, zoom in on flaws in tagged photos, and ask my friends for reassurance that I didn't look terrible.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid social or work situations, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in general, or because you are preoccupied with a specific flaw in your appearance even when other people say they do not notice it?\n\nPatient: My avoidance is entirely because of my preoccupation with my dark circles and puffy face, not a general fear of embarrassment. Even though my coworkers and friends say they don't notice anything, I'm convinced I look bloated and exhausted on camera, which makes me want to hide and avoid being seen or photographed at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social or work situations mainly because you fear being judged or embarrassed by other people in general, or is it mostly because of a specific concern about your appearance even when others say they do not notice a problem?\n\nPatient: It is definitely the latter, as my avoidance is entirely driven by my obsession with how my face looks, specifically my dark circles and bloated appearance. Even when my friends and colleagues reassure me that they don't notice anything wrong, I still can't stop fixating on these flaws. That is why I avoid these situations, rather than having a general fear of people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the problem is more about being judged or embarrassed in social situations in general, even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only about the specific flaw in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, it is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance, like my bloated face and dark circles. I actually don't have a general fear of social situations at all—I used to be very active with dancing and soccer, and I only started skipping those outings because I'm terrified of being photographed or tagged on social media.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that you have a serious medical or physical illness because of these appearance concerns, or is the problem mainly that you think your appearance is flawed and others will notice it?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness; the issue is purely aesthetic. I am just deeply self-conscious about looking bloated and uneven with these dark circles, and I constantly worry that others will notice and judge me for looking exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid social situations because you feel awkward or afraid of being judged even when your appearance is not the focus, or is it only because of the specific flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel awkward or anxious in social situations for any other reason; it is strictly because of how I look. I only avoid these events because I am terrified of people seeing my bloated face and dark circles, or being captured in an unflattering photo.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid events, is it mainly because you fear being embarrassed or judged in social situations in general, or is it almost entirely because you are preoccupied with the specific appearance flaws you see in your face?\n\nPatient: It is almost entirely because of my preoccupation with my specific flaws, like my dark circles and bloated, uneven face, and the dread of being captured on camera. I don't have a general fear of social situations at all. I just can't bear the thought of people seeing or photographing these parts of my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to others, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that the dark circles and the bloated, uneven look of my face are very real and highly visible. I just can't help but feel that everyone is secretly judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a strong fear of being embarrassed or judged in social situations even when your appearance is not being noticed, or is your distress only about the specific flaw you see in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, my distress is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance. I don't have a general fear of being judged or embarrassed in social situations at all. My anxiety and avoidance, like skipping soccer or parties, are purely driven by the fear of being photographed or having others notice my dark circles and bloated face.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious illness or disease because of these symptoms, or is your concern mainly that your appearance looks flawed or unattractive?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness or disease at all. My concern is entirely that my appearance looks flawed, bloated, and uneven, which makes me look incredibly unattractive and exhausted on camera. I am just deeply distressed by how I look in photos, not by any fear of being sick.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is even a small chance that your concern about your face may be exaggerated or not as visible to others as it feels to you, or are you completely certain the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my bloated face and dark circles are real and highly obvious. When I look in the mirror or on camera, it feels absolutely undeniable, and I often worry that others are just trying to be nice when they say they don't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance that your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to other people, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though others tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my dark circles and bloated face are real and highly obvious. I constantly replay those images in my head, certain that everyone thinks I look exhausted and sloppy, which is why I keep asking my friends for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that other people are deliberately talking about, mocking, or targeting you because of your appearance, or is your concern limited to being very distressed about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking or targeting me maliciously. I am just constantly convinced that anyone looking at me must be thinking I look exhausted, bloated, and sloppy because of my dark circles and uneven face. My distress is entirely about those specific flaws and the fear of being judged for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that these appearance concerns might be exaggerated or not actually visible to other people, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?"

I don't have a general fear of social situations at all. I just can't bear the thought of people seeing or photographing these parts of my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to others, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that the dark circles and the bloated, uneven look of my face are very real and highly visible. I just can't help but feel that everyone is secretly judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have a strong fear of being embarrassed or judged in social situations even when your appearance is not being noticed, or is your distress only about the specific flaw you see in your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, my distress is strictly about the specific flaws in my appearance. I don't have a general fear of being judged or embarrassed in social situations at all. My anxiety and avoidance, like skipping soccer or parties, are purely driven by the fear of being photographed or having others notice my dark circles and bloated face.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying that you have a serious illness or disease because of these symptoms, or is your concern mainly that your appearance looks flawed or unattractive?\n\nPatient: No, I don't worry about having a medical illness or disease at all. My concern is entirely that my appearance looks flawed, bloated, and uneven, which makes me look incredibly unattractive and exhausted on camera. I am just deeply distressed by how I look in photos, not by any fear of being sick.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is even a small chance that your concern about your face may be exaggerated or not as visible to others as it feels to you, or are you completely certain the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though my friends and coworkers tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my bloated face and dark circles are real and highly obvious. When I look in the mirror or on camera, it feels absolutely undeniable, and I often worry that others are just trying to be nice when they say they don't see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is a chance that your concern about your appearance might be exaggerated or not actually visible to other people, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: Even though others tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my dark circles and bloated face are real and highly obvious. I constantly replay those images in my head, certain that everyone thinks I look exhausted and sloppy, which is why I keep asking my friends for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that other people are deliberately talking about, mocking, or targeting you because of your appearance, or is your concern limited to being very distressed about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking or targeting me maliciously. I am just constantly convinced that anyone looking at me must be thinking I look exhausted, bloated, and sloppy because of my dark circles and uneven face. My distress is entirely about those specific flaws and the fear of being judged for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that these appearance concerns might be exaggerated or not actually visible to other people, or are you completely certain that the flaw is real and obvious?\n\nPatient: I am completely convinced that these flaws—specifically my dark circles and puffy, uneven face—are real and highly obvious, even if my friends and coworkers say they don't notice anything. I just can't help but feel that everyone is secretly judging me and thinking I look exhausted and sloppy when they see me."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, I don't worry about having a medical illness; the issue is purely aesthetic. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nEven though others tell me they don't notice anything wrong, I am completely convinced that my dark circles and bloated face are real and highly obvious.
